Hi, doctor. Yeah, um... well, the main reason I’m here is that I'm having a really hard time at work. I'm a registered nurse, and on the floor, I just lose my train of thought constantly. It’s so frustrating. Like, our unit is super busy—we actually just got a new nurse manager, and she’s been changing all the scheduling rules, which is a whole other headache because I usually like doing three 12-hour shifts in a row, but she wants us to break them up now. And driving in this morning, the traffic was terrible because of some construction on I-95, so I was already stressed about being late to this appointment...

It's just... if an IV pump beeps, or I overhear someone asking another nurse a question, or a call light goes off down the hall, suddenly I’ve completely forgotten what patient flow issue I was even working on. It's like my brain just wipes completely blank.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, um... well, the main reason I’m here is that I'm having a really hard time at work. I'm a registered nurse, and on the floor, I just lose my train of thought constantly. It’s so frustrating. Right, work. It's just... if an IV pump beeps, or I overhear someone asking another nurse a question, or a call light goes off down the hall, suddenly I’ve completely forgotten what patient flow issue I was even working on. I miss her so much. The new manager, the one who's messing with our schedules, she doesn't do anything like that. She's all about \"efficiency metrics\" and whatever. They have these huge, thick soles. Everyone on the unit wears them now. \n\nI used to be a strict Dansko clog girl, but they're just so heavy, and my dog actually chewed the heel off my last pair. He’s a golden retriever mix, total menace when he's bored. We try to take him to the dog park on weekends to tire him out, but it's been raining so much lately that it's just a mud pit. But anyway, the Hokas were like a hundred and sixty dollars, which is crazy for work shoes, but... wait, I'm sorry. What did you just ask me? \n\nOh, right, acting without thinking. No, I don't really do that. I wouldn't say I'm impulsive or fidgety. I just... I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My mom had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to keep me on track. 

Oh, our kitchen table actually had this huge scratch right down the middle of it from when my brother tried to build a birdhouse inside. He was always doing stuff like that—he's an engineer now, actually, living out in Seattle. I went to visit him last fall, and the coffee out there is amazing. We went to this little roastery near Pike Place Market, and I bought three bags of beans to bring home, but then I left them in the rental car at the airport. I was so mad at myself. The rental car lady was really nice about it when I called, but they never found them. 

But yeah, the focus thing... it's definitely not new. It's just that the charge nurse stuff made it impossible to hide anymore.

Has this always been something you've struggled with, or is this relatively new for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a little scatterbrained, I guess, but it's gotten so much worse lately. Like, it became a really major problem when I took on these expanded charge-nurse responsibilities. That was almost two years ago now. Our old manager, Linda, she practically begged me to take the role before she retired. She was great, by the way—she always brought in donuts on Fridays from this little bakery near her house. I miss her so much. The new manager, the one who's messing with our schedules, she doesn't do anything like that. She's all about \"efficiency metrics\" and whatever. They have these huge, thick soles. Everyone on the unit wears them now. \n\nI used to be a strict Dansko clog girl, but they're just so heavy, and my dog actually chewed the heel off my last pair. He’s a golden retriever mix, total menace when he's bored. We try to take him to the dog park on weekends to tire him out, but it's been raining so much lately that it's just a mud pit. I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My mom had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to keep me on track. \n\nOh, our kitchen table actually had this huge scratch right down the middle of it from when my brother tried to build a birdhouse inside. He was always doing stuff like that—he's an engineer now, actually, living out in Seattle. I went to visit him last fall, and the coffee out there is amazing. We went to this little roastery near Pike Place Market, and I bought three bags of beans to bring home, but then I left them in the rental car at the airport. I was so mad at myself. The rental car lady was really nice about it when I called, but they never found them. \n\nBut yeah, the focus thing... it's definitely not new. Um, no, not really. I was never the hyper kid. The teachers never said I was bouncing off the walls or anything like that. It was always just the daydreaming, losing my pencils, that kind of thing. I actually loved school supplies, though. Every August, my mom would take me to Staples, and I'd pick out those Lisa Frank folders. Do you remember those? With the neon dolphins and the golden retrievers? They were so cool. I had this one pencil case that was holographic, and I kept all my gel pens in it. Milky pens were the best, especially the pastel ones on black paper. I used to write notes to my best friend Sarah in class using those. 

Sarah actually just had her second baby, a little boy. I went to her baby shower last month, and they had this incredible diaper cake. I don't know who makes those, but it must take hours to roll all those diapers up. I bought her a bunch of onesies with little ducks on them because her nursery theme is ponds and frogs and stuff. It's really cute, but I almost didn't make it to the shower because I wrote down the wrong time in my planner. I use a paper planner because if I put it in my phone, it just disappears into the void, you know? But even with the paper one, I still mess things up. 

But anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, being hyper. No, I wasn't hyper. I would just sit there quietly, but my brain would be a million miles away, thinking about gel pens or whatever.

I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My mom had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to keep me on track. \n\nOh, our kitchen table actually had this huge scratch right down the middle of it from when my brother tried to build a birdhouse inside. He was always doing stuff like that—he's an engineer now, actually, living out in Seattle. I went to visit him last fall, and the coffee out there is amazing. We went to this little roastery near Pike Place Market, and I bought three bags of beans to bring home, but then I left them in the rental car at the airport. I was so mad at myself. The rental car lady was really nice about it when I called, but they never found them. \n\nBut yeah, the focus thing... it's definitely not new. It's just that the charge nurse stuff made it impossible to hide anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, around ages 6 to 12, were you having trouble paying attention and staying organized even back then, or do you remember doing okay with focus during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely happening back then too, right around those ages. My teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was, you know, bright but careless. I'd lose my worksheets constantly, or I'd do the homework and just completely forget to turn it in. It drove my mom crazy. She had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to make sure I actually stayed on track and finished it. \n\nShe was so patient, honestly. My oven is so weird anyway, it runs really hot because it's one of those old gas ones. The landlord said he'd replace it when I moved in, but he's terrible at getting back to me. I've texted him like three times about the leaky faucet in the bathroom, too. It just drips all night. The ocean waves setting is my favorite, but the thunderstorm one is just... \n\nWait. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. Or were the main complaints just about not paying attention and losing things?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I was never the hyper kid. The teachers never said I was bouncing off the walls or anything like that. It was always just the daydreaming, losing my pencils, that kind of thing. I actually loved school supplies, though. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I really didn't have trouble sitting still. Like I was saying, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit at my desk, totally zoned out. We had these desks where the top lifted up, and you could keep all your stuff inside them. I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. There were always crumpled up papers and broken crayons at the bottom. 

Crayola crayons were the best, though. The off-brand ones always felt super waxy and didn't color right. My grandma used to keep a huge tin of broken crayons at her house for when my cousins and I came over. She lived in this old Victorian house that always smelled like peppermint and floor wax. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. I actually inherited a smaller clock from her when she passed away, but I haven't been able to get it to work. I think the pendulum is off-balance or something. I keep meaning to take it to a repair shop, but I always forget. There's a guy downtown who fixes watches and old clocks, right next to that amazing bakery that makes those massive cinnamon rolls. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I went there last weekend and got a box of four, but I left them on the roof of my car while I was unlocking the door and just drove away. I saw them fly off in my rearview mirror. It was tragic. 

But um, yeah, sitting still. I was totally fine sitting still. I just wasn't mentally in the room half the time.

I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. There were always crumpled up papers and broken crayons at the bottom. \n\nCrayola crayons were the best, though. The off-brand ones always felt super waxy and didn't color right. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. Or was it mainly just a school problem?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / registered_nurse / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, it was definitely at home, too. It wasn't just school. I'd lose my toys constantly. My mom would ask me to go clean my room, and I'd go in there, pick up a Barbie, and then just start playing with it and completely forget I was supposed to be cleaning. I had this huge Barbie Dreamhouse—the one with the elevator you had to pull a string to make it go up and down. The string broke after like a month, though, and my dad tried to fix it with some fishing line, but it never really worked the same. 

My dad loves fishing, actually. He goes up to this lake in Wisconsin every summer with his buddies. They rent this little cabin that doesn't even have Wi-Fi, which sounds so relaxing to me right now. The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. I use this giant clipboard with all these different colored sticky notes just to keep everything straight, but sometimes the notes fall off. Last week, I found one stuck to the bottom of my shoe when I got home. 

Oh! And I have to buy these specific nursing shoes because I'm on my feet for twelve hours, and my arches are terrible. I ordered a new pair online, but I put in my old apartment address by accident. I had to drive over there to see if the new tenants had my package, and they were so weird about it. They had this massive dog that just kept barking at me through the window. It looked like a Mastiff mix or something. I love dogs, but big ones kind of freak me out ever since I got knocked over by a Golden Retriever at the park when I was little. 

Wait, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, losing things and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing... it was definitely a problem at home and with friends, too, not just at school.

I had this one pencil case that was holographic, and I kept all my gel pens in it. Milky pens were the best, especially the pastel ones on black paper. I used to write notes to my best friend Sarah in class using those. \n\nSarah actually just had her second baby, a little boy. I went to her baby shower last month, and they had this incredible diaper cake. I don't know who makes those, but it must take hours to roll all those diapers up. It's really cute, but I almost didn't make it to the shower because I wrote down the wrong time in my planner. I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. There were always crumpled up papers and broken crayons at the bottom. \n\nCrayola crayons were the best, though. The off-brand ones always felt super waxy and didn't color right. My grandma used to keep a huge tin of broken crayons at her house for when my cousins and I came over. She lived in this old Victorian house that always smelled like peppermint and floor wax. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. I actually inherited a smaller clock from her when she passed away, but I haven't been able to get it to work. I think the pendulum is off-balance or something. I keep meaning to take it to a repair shop, but I always forget. There's a guy downtown who fixes watches and old clocks, right next to that amazing bakery that makes those massive cinnamon rolls. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I had this huge Barbie Dreamhouse—the one with the elevator you had to pull a string to make it go up and down. The string broke after like a month, though, and my dad tried to fix it with some fishing line, but it never really worked the same. \n\nMy dad loves fishing, actually. He goes up to this lake in Wisconsin every summer with his buddies. They rent this little cabin that doesn't even have Wi-Fi, which sounds so relaxing to me right now. The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. Um, it’s everywhere, honestly, but work is where it’s really crashing down on me right now. The concerns there have gotten serious enough that I’m actually on formal supervision. The other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's humiliating. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, you know? 

Brenda is usually the one who has to check my work. She’s super sweet about it, but I still hate it. She actually just got back from an Alaskan cruise last week and brought some smoked salmon into the break room. She showed me all these pictures of the glaciers from her balcony, and it looked absolutely freezing. I don't think I could ever do a cold-weather vacation. I despise being cold. I actually bought this massive, heavy-duty down puffer coat last winter from REI—like, the kind that goes all the way down to your ankles—just because I got assigned to the satellite parking lot at the hospital and have to wait outside. 

You have to take this little shuttle bus from the lot to the main entrance, and the heater on it is always broken. The driver, Gary, is hilarious, though. He always wears these vintage aviator sunglasses and plays classic rock on the radio at like, six in the morning. We were listening to Fleetwood Mac the other day. *Rumours* is probably one of the best albums ever recorded. My dad had it on vinyl when I was growing up, and I always loved the album cover with that weird little crystal ball thing... 

Wait, I'm sorry, I'm totally rambling. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's just the nursing job. I mean, it messes up my personal life too, but work is just the most stressful part right now because of the whole supervision thing.

Oh, no, it’s definitely not manageable at home either. It’s honestly a disaster everywhere. I constantly forget things that should just be completely routine. Like, I’ll completely forget to pay a bill at home, or I'll forget to call a family member back. My sister got so mad at me last week because I didn't call her back about planning her baby shower. She's having a boy, which is super exciting because they already have two girls. 

The girls are adorable, they do competitive cheerleading now. I did gymnastics when I was little, but I had to quit because I broke my wrist falling off the balance beam. I had to wear this neon pink cast for like six weeks in the third grade, and it got so gross and itchy inside. My mom used to let me stick a long knitting needle down there to scratch it, which you are definitely not supposed to do—as a nurse, I know that now. I actually had a patient come up to the med-surg unit with a fresh cast a few weeks ago, and it totally brought me back to that weird fiberglass smell. 

But um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my personal life and forgetting things. Yeah, it's bad. I'll forget to follow up on a missing order, or I'll just completely fail to show up for a doctor's appointment or a haircut. And the most frustrating part is that I really meant to do it! I'll even set a reminder on my phone, and it just doesn't matter. The reminder goes off, I swipe it away thinking, 'I'll do that in literally two seconds,' and then my brain just totally deletes it.

He’s a golden retriever mix, total menace when he's bored. We try to take him to the dog park on weekends to tire him out, but it's been raining so much lately that it's just a mud pit. But anyway, the Hokas were like a hundred and sixty dollars, which is crazy for work shoes, but... wait, I'm sorry. What did you just ask me? \n\nOh, right, acting without thinking. No, I don't really do that. I wouldn't say I'm impulsive or fidgety. I just... I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My mom had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to keep me on track. \n\nOh, our kitchen table actually had this huge scratch right down the middle of it from when my brother tried to build a birdhouse inside. He was always doing stuff like that—he's an engineer now, actually, living out in Seattle. I went to visit him last fall, and the coffee out there is amazing. We went to this little roastery near Pike Place Market, and I bought three bags of beans to bring home, but then I left them in the rental car at the airport. I was so mad at myself. The rental car lady was really nice about it when I called, but they never found them. \n\nBut yeah, the focus thing... it's definitely not new. It's just that the charge nurse stuff made it impossible to hide anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, around ages 6 to 12, were you having trouble paying attention and staying organized even back then, or do you remember doing okay with focus during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely happening back then too, right around those ages. My teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was, you know, bright but careless. I'd lose my worksheets constantly, or I'd do the homework and just completely forget to turn it in. It drove my mom crazy. She had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to make sure I actually stayed on track and finished it. \n\nShe was so patient, honestly. She used to make this amazing pot roast on those nights, actually. I tried making it last week for my boyfriend, but I totally burned it because I got distracted and forgot to set a timer. My oven is so weird anyway, it runs really hot because it's one of those old gas ones. The landlord said he'd replace it when I moved in, but he's terrible at getting back to me. I've texted him like three times about the leaky faucet in the bathroom, too. It just drips all night. I actually had to buy a white noise machine on Amazon just to sleep because the sound was driving me insane. Have you ever used one of those? The ocean waves setting is my favorite, but the thunderstorm one is just... \n\nWait. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But yeah, to answer your question, elementary school was definitely a struggle with the focus and organization, even way back then.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your mom ever say you were too hyper, couldn't sit still, or were always fidgeting and interrupting people? Or were the main complaints just about not paying attention and losing things?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I was never the hyper kid. The teachers never said I was bouncing off the walls or anything like that. It was always just the daydreaming, losing my pencils, that kind of thing. I actually loved school supplies, though. Every August, my mom would take me to Staples, and I'd pick out those Lisa Frank folders. Do you remember those? With the neon dolphins and the golden retrievers? They were so cool. I had this one pencil case that was holographic, and I kept all my gel pens in it. Milky pens were the best, especially the pastel ones on black paper. I used to write notes to my best friend Sarah in class using those. \n\nSarah actually just had her second baby, a little boy. I went to her baby shower last month, and they had this incredible diaper cake. I don't know who makes those, but it must take hours to roll all those diapers up. I bought her a bunch of onesies with little ducks on them because her nursery theme is ponds and frogs and stuff. It's really cute, but I almost didn't make it to the shower because I wrote down the wrong time in my planner. I use a paper planner because if I put it in my phone, it just disappears into the void, you know? But even with the paper one, I still mess things up. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, being hyper. No, I wasn't hyper. I would just sit there quietly, but my brain would be a million miles away, thinking about gel pens or whatever.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did you ever have trouble sitting still in class, or were you able to sit quietly at your desk without much problem?\n\nPatient: No, I really didn't have trouble sitting still. Like I was saying, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit at my desk, totally zoned out. We had these desks where the top lifted up, and you could keep all your stuff inside them. I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. There were always crumpled up papers and broken crayons at the bottom. \n\nCrayola crayons were the best, though. The off-brand ones always felt super waxy and didn't color right. My grandma used to keep a huge tin of broken crayons at her house for when my cousins and I came over. She lived in this old Victorian house that always smelled like peppermint and floor wax. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. I actually inherited a smaller clock from her when she passed away, but I haven't been able to get it to work. I think the pendulum is off-balance or something. I keep meaning to take it to a repair shop, but I always forget. There's a guy downtown who fixes watches and old clocks, right next to that amazing bakery that makes those massive cinnamon rolls. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I went there last weekend and got a box of four, but I left them on the roof of my car while I was unlocking the door and just drove away. I saw them fly off in my rearview mirror. It was tragic. \n\nBut um, yeah, sitting still. I was totally fine sitting still. I just wasn't mentally in the room half the time.\n\nDoctor: Besides school, when you were a kid at home or playing with friends, did you also have trouble keeping track of things, losing your belongings, or forgetting what you were supposed to be doing? Or was it mainly just a school problem?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it was definitely at home, too. It wasn't just school. I'd lose my toys constantly. My mom would ask me to go clean my room, and I'd go in there, pick up a Barbie, and then just start playing with it and completely forget I was supposed to be cleaning. I had this huge Barbie Dreamhouse—the one with the elevator you had to pull a string to make it go up and down. The string broke after like a month, though, and my dad tried to fix it with some fishing line, but it never really worked the same. \n\nMy dad loves fishing, actually. He goes up to this lake in Wisconsin every summer with his buddies. They rent this little cabin that doesn't even have Wi-Fi, which sounds so relaxing to me right now. The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. I use this giant clipboard with all these different colored sticky notes just to keep everything straight, but sometimes the notes fall off. Last week, I found one stuck to the bottom of my shoe when I got home. \n\nOh! And I have to buy these specific nursing shoes because I'm on my feet for twelve hours, and my arches are terrible. I ordered a new pair online, but I put in my old apartment address by accident. I had to drive over there to see if the new tenants had my package, and they were so weird about it. They had this massive dog that just kept barking at me through the window. It looked like a Mastiff mix or something. I love dogs, but big ones kind of freak me out ever since I got knocked over by a Golden Retriever at the park when I was little. \n\nWait, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, losing things and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing... it was definitely a problem at home and with friends, too, not just at school.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and back in school. Looking at your life overall right now—not just work—would you say these attention and organization problems are causing you real difficulties in multiple areas, or is it mainly just the nursing job that's been affected?\n\nPatient: Um, it’s everywhere, honestly, but work is where it’s really crashing down on me right now. The concerns there have gotten serious enough that I’m actually on formal supervision. The other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's humiliating. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, you know? \n\nBrenda is usually the one who has to check my work. She’s super sweet about it, but I still hate it. She actually just got back from an Alaskan cruise last week and brought some smoked salmon into the break room. She showed me all these pictures of the glaciers from her balcony, and it looked absolutely freezing. I don't think I could ever do a cold-weather vacation. I despise being cold. I actually bought this massive, heavy-duty down puffer coat last winter from REI—like, the kind that goes all the way down to your ankles—just because I got assigned to the satellite parking lot at the hospital and have to wait outside. \n\nYou have to take this little shuttle bus from the lot to the main entrance, and the heater on it is always broken. The driver, Gary, is hilarious, though. He always wears these vintage aviator sunglasses and plays classic rock on the radio at like, six in the morning. We were listening to Fleetwood Mac the other day. *Rumours* is probably one of the best albums ever recorded. My dad had it on vinyl when I was growing up, and I always loved the album cover with that weird little crystal ball thing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm totally rambling. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's just the nursing job. I mean, it messes up my personal life too, but work is just the most stressful part right now because of the whole supervision thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the work supervision is really stressful. Outside of work—at home, in your relationships, managing your finances, or other personal responsibilities—are you having significant problems because of losing track of things and forgetting what you need to do, or is it more manageable in those areas?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s definitely not manageable at home either. It’s honestly a disaster everywhere. I constantly forget things that should just be completely routine. Like, I’ll completely forget to pay a bill at home, or I'll forget to call a family member back. My sister got so mad at me last week because I didn't call her back about planning her baby shower. She's having a boy, which is super exciting because they already have two girls. \n\nThe girls are adorable, they do competitive cheerleading now. I did gymnastics when I was little, but I had to quit because I broke my wrist falling off the balance beam. I had to wear this neon pink cast for like six weeks in the third grade, and it got so gross and itchy inside. My mom used to let me stick a long knitting needle down there to scratch it, which you are definitely not supposed to do—as a nurse, I know that now. I actually had a patient come up to the med-surg unit with a fresh cast a few weeks ago, and it totally brought me back to that weird fiberglass smell. \n\nBut um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my personal life and forgetting things. Yeah, it's bad. I'll forget to follow up on a missing order, or I'll just completely fail to show up for a doctor's appointment or a haircut. And the most frustrating part is that I really meant to do it! I'll even set a reminder on my phone, and it just doesn't matter. The reminder goes off, I swipe it away thinking, 'I'll do that in literally two seconds,' and then my brain just totally deletes it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I don't get that antsy feeling where I need to move around. I can sit still for a movie or whatever. I actually love just sitting on the couch. We just watched this huge marathon of those baking shows on Netflix last weekend. My boyfriend loves them, but he always falls asleep halfway through the episodes. He works construction, so he's up at like 4 AM every day, which is just insane to me. I absolutely need my sleep. 

We actually just bought a new mattress because our old one was sagging so badly in the middle and it was making my lower back hurt on top of being on my feet all day at the hospital. It's one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box. It's crazy how they vacuum seal them! You cut the plastic and it just expands like a giant sponge. The delivery guy left it out in the rain, though, and the cardboard got completely mushy. Our front porch doesn't have an awning, which is something I really want to get fixed before winter. I saw this really cute striped one on Pinterest that would match our shutters perfectly, but I don't know how much it costs to install something like that... 

Wait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, getting up and moving around. No, I don't really have a problem with sitting still. I'm usually perfectly fine just staying put.

My grandma used to keep a huge tin of broken crayons at her house for when my cousins and I came over. She lived in this old Victorian house that always smelled like peppermint and floor wax. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. I actually inherited a smaller clock from her when she passed away, but I haven't been able to get it to work. I think the pendulum is off-balance or something. I keep meaning to take it to a repair shop, but I always forget. There's a guy downtown who fixes watches and old clocks, right next to that amazing bakery that makes those massive cinnamon rolls. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I went there last weekend and got a box of four, but I left them on the roof of my car while I was unlocking the door and just drove away. I saw them fly off in my rearview mirror. It was tragic. \n\nBut um, yeah, sitting still. I was totally fine sitting still. I just wasn't mentally in the room half the time.\n\nDoctor: Besides school, when you were a kid at home or playing with friends, did you also have trouble keeping track of things, losing your belongings, or forgetting what you were supposed to be doing? Or was it mainly just a school problem?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it was definitely at home, too. It wasn't just school. I'd lose my toys constantly. My mom would ask me to go clean my room, and I'd go in there, pick up a Barbie, and then just start playing with it and completely forget I was supposed to be cleaning. I had this huge Barbie Dreamhouse—the one with the elevator you had to pull a string to make it go up and down. The string broke after like a month, though, and my dad tried to fix it with some fishing line, but it never really worked the same. \n\nMy dad loves fishing, actually. He goes up to this lake in Wisconsin every summer with his buddies. They rent this little cabin that doesn't even have Wi-Fi, which sounds so relaxing to me right now. The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. I use this giant clipboard with all these different colored sticky notes just to keep everything straight, but sometimes the notes fall off. Last week, I found one stuck to the bottom of my shoe when I got home. \n\nOh! And I have to buy these specific nursing shoes because I'm on my feet for twelve hours, and my arches are terrible. I ordered a new pair online, but I put in my old apartment address by accident. I had to drive over there to see if the new tenants had my package, and they were so weird about it. They had this massive dog that just kept barking at me through the window. It looked like a Mastiff mix or something. I love dogs, but big ones kind of freak me out ever since I got knocked over by a Golden Retriever at the park when I was little. \n\nWait, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, losing things and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing... it was definitely a problem at home and with friends, too, not just at school.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and back in school. Looking at your life overall right now—not just work—would you say these attention and organization problems are causing you real difficulties in multiple areas, or is it mainly just the nursing job that's been affected?\n\nPatient: Um, it’s everywhere, honestly, but work is where it’s really crashing down on me right now. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, you know? \n\nBrenda is usually the one who has to check my work. She’s super sweet about it, but I still hate it. She actually just got back from an Alaskan cruise last week and brought some smoked salmon into the break room. She showed me all these pictures of the glaciers from her balcony, and it looked absolutely freezing. I don't think I could ever do a cold-weather vacation. I despise being cold. I actually bought this massive, heavy-duty down puffer coat last winter from REI—like, the kind that goes all the way down to your ankles—just because I got assigned to the satellite parking lot at the hospital and have to wait outside. \n\nYou have to take this little shuttle bus from the lot to the main entrance, and the heater on it is always broken. The driver, Gary, is hilarious, though. He always wears these vintage aviator sunglasses and plays classic rock on the radio at like, six in the morning. We were listening to Fleetwood Mac the other day. *Rumours* is probably one of the best albums ever recorded. My dad had it on vinyl when I was growing up, and I always loved the album cover with that weird little crystal ball thing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm totally rambling. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's just the nursing job. I mean, it messes up my personal life too, but work is just the most stressful part right now because of the whole supervision thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the work supervision is really stressful. It’s honestly a disaster everywhere. I constantly forget things that should just be completely routine. She's having a boy, which is super exciting because they already have two girls. \n\nThe girls are adorable, they do competitive cheerleading now. I did gymnastics when I was little, but I had to quit because I broke my wrist falling off the balance beam. The reminder goes off, I swipe it away thinking, 'I'll do that in literally two seconds,' and then my brain just totally deletes it.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on something—like reading a book, watching a movie, or having a conversation—do you find yourself feeling antsy or like you need to get up and move around, even if there's no real reason to?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't get that antsy feeling where I need to move around. I can sit still for a movie or whatever. I actually love just sitting on the couch. I absolutely need my sleep. \n\nWe actually just bought a new mattress because our old one was sagging so badly in the middle and it was making my lower back hurt on top of being on my feet all day at the hospital. Well, like I said before, the work thing is pretty major. I haven't actually been fired, but I am on that formal supervision right now. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, um... well, the main reason I’m here is that I'm having a really hard time at work. I'm a registered nurse, and on the floor, I just lose my train of thought constantly. It’s so frustrating. Like, our unit is super busy—we actually just got a new nurse manager, and she’s been changing all the scheduling rules, which is a whole other headache because I usually like doing three 12-hour shifts in a row, but she wants us to break them up now. And driving in this morning, the traffic was terrible because of some construction on I-95, so I was already stressed about being late to this appointment... \n\nBut anyway, wait, what was I saying? Right, work. It's just... if an IV pump beeps, or I overhear someone asking another nurse a question, or a call light goes off down the hall, suddenly I’ve completely forgotten what patient flow issue I was even working on. It's like my brain just wipes completely blank.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with losing your train of thought and getting distracted? Has this always been something you've struggled with, or is this relatively new for you?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a little scatterbrained, I guess, but it's gotten so much worse lately. Like, it became a really major problem when I took on these expanded charge-nurse responsibilities. That was almost two years ago now. Our old manager, Linda, she practically begged me to take the role before she retired. She was great, by the way—she always brought in donuts on Fridays from this little bakery near her house. I miss her so much. The new manager, the one who's messing with our schedules, she doesn't do anything like that. She's all about \"efficiency metrics\" and whatever. But yeah, ever since I started doing charge, which is a lot more coordinating and less direct patient care, my brain just... I don't know, it just can't hold onto anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble focusing and losing your train of thought, do you find yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or like you need to be moving around? Or do you act without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't feel like I have to be moving, but I mean, as a nurse, I'm literally always walking anyway. I usually hit like ten thousand steps before lunch. Actually, my feet were hurting so bad last month that I finally caved and bought a pair of those Hoka sneakers. Have you seen them? They have these huge, thick soles. Everyone on the unit wears them now. \n\nI used to be a strict Dansko clog girl, but they're just so heavy, and my dog actually chewed the heel off my last pair. He’s a golden retriever mix, total menace when he's bored. We try to take him to the dog park on weekends to tire him out, but it's been raining so much lately that it's just a mud pit. But anyway, the Hokas were like a hundred and sixty dollars, which is crazy for work shoes, but... wait, I'm sorry. What did you just ask me? \n\nOh, right, acting without thinking. No, I don't really do that. I wouldn't say I'm impulsive or fidgety. I just... I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My mom had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to keep me on track. \n\nOh, our kitchen table actually had this huge scratch right down the middle of it from when my brother tried to build a birdhouse inside. He was always doing stuff like that—he's an engineer now, actually, living out in Seattle. I went to visit him last fall, and the coffee out there is amazing. We went to this little roastery near Pike Place Market, and I bought three bags of beans to bring home, but then I left them in the rental car at the airport. I was so mad at myself. The rental car lady was really nice about it when I called, but they never found them. \n\nBut yeah, the focus thing... it's definitely not new. It's just that the charge nurse stuff made it impossible to hide anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, around ages 6 to 12, were you having trouble paying attention and staying organized even back then, or do you remember doing okay with focus during that time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely happening back then too, right around those ages. My teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was, you know, bright but careless. I'd lose my worksheets constantly, or I'd do the homework and just completely forget to turn it in. It drove my mom crazy. She had to sit with me at the kitchen table every single night just to make sure I actually stayed on track and finished it. \n\nShe was so patient, honestly. She used to make this amazing pot roast on those nights, actually. I tried making it last week for my boyfriend, but I totally burned it because I got distracted and forgot to set a timer. My oven is so weird anyway, it runs really hot because it's one of those old gas ones. The landlord said he'd replace it when I moved in, but he's terrible at getting back to me. I've texted him like three times about the leaky faucet in the bathroom, too. It just drips all night. I actually had to buy a white noise machine on Amazon just to sleep because the sound was driving me insane. Have you ever used one of those? The ocean waves setting is my favorite, but the thunderstorm one is just... \n\nWait. I'm doing it again, aren't I? I'm sorry. But yeah, to answer your question, elementary school was definitely a struggle with the focus and organization, even way back then.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did teachers or your mom ever say you were too hyper, couldn't sit still, or were always fidgeting and interrupting people? Or were the main complaints just about not paying attention and losing things?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I was never the hyper kid. The teachers never said I was bouncing off the walls or anything like that. It was always just the daydreaming, losing my pencils, that kind of thing. I actually loved school supplies, though. Every August, my mom would take me to Staples, and I'd pick out those Lisa Frank folders. Do you remember those? With the neon dolphins and the golden retrievers? They were so cool. I had this one pencil case that was holographic, and I kept all my gel pens in it. Milky pens were the best, especially the pastel ones on black paper. I used to write notes to my best friend Sarah in class using those. \n\nSarah actually just had her second baby, a little boy. I went to her baby shower last month, and they had this incredible diaper cake. I don't know who makes those, but it must take hours to roll all those diapers up. I bought her a bunch of onesies with little ducks on them because her nursery theme is ponds and frogs and stuff. It's really cute, but I almost didn't make it to the shower because I wrote down the wrong time in my planner. I use a paper planner because if I put it in my phone, it just disappears into the void, you know? But even with the paper one, I still mess things up. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, being hyper. No, I wasn't hyper. I would just sit there quietly, but my brain would be a million miles away, thinking about gel pens or whatever.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did you ever have trouble sitting still in class, or were you able to sit quietly at your desk without much problem?\n\nPatient: No, I really didn't have trouble sitting still. Like I was saying, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit at my desk, totally zoned out. We had these desks where the top lifted up, and you could keep all your stuff inside them. I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. There were always crumpled up papers and broken crayons at the bottom. \n\nCrayola crayons were the best, though. The off-brand ones always felt super waxy and didn't color right. My grandma used to keep a huge tin of broken crayons at her house for when my cousins and I came over. She lived in this old Victorian house that always smelled like peppermint and floor wax. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. I actually inherited a smaller clock from her when she passed away, but I haven't been able to get it to work. I think the pendulum is off-balance or something. I keep meaning to take it to a repair shop, but I always forget. There's a guy downtown who fixes watches and old clocks, right next to that amazing bakery that makes those massive cinnamon rolls. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I went there last weekend and got a box of four, but I left them on the roof of my car while I was unlocking the door and just drove away. I saw them fly off in my rearview mirror. It was tragic. \n\nBut um, yeah, sitting still. I was totally fine sitting still. I just wasn't mentally in the room half the time.\n\nDoctor: Besides school, when you were a kid at home or playing with friends, did you also have trouble keeping track of things, losing your belongings, or forgetting what you were supposed to be doing? Or was it mainly just a school problem?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it was definitely at home, too. It wasn't just school. I'd lose my toys constantly. My mom would ask me to go clean my room, and I'd go in there, pick up a Barbie, and then just start playing with it and completely forget I was supposed to be cleaning. I had this huge Barbie Dreamhouse—the one with the elevator you had to pull a string to make it go up and down. The string broke after like a month, though, and my dad tried to fix it with some fishing line, but it never really worked the same. \n\nMy dad loves fishing, actually. He goes up to this lake in Wisconsin every summer with his buddies. They rent this little cabin that doesn't even have Wi-Fi, which sounds so relaxing to me right now. The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. I use this giant clipboard with all these different colored sticky notes just to keep everything straight, but sometimes the notes fall off. Last week, I found one stuck to the bottom of my shoe when I got home. \n\nOh! And I have to buy these specific nursing shoes because I'm on my feet for twelve hours, and my arches are terrible. I ordered a new pair online, but I put in my old apartment address by accident. I had to drive over there to see if the new tenants had my package, and they were so weird about it. They had this massive dog that just kept barking at me through the window. It looked like a Mastiff mix or something. I love dogs, but big ones kind of freak me out ever since I got knocked over by a Golden Retriever at the park when I was little. \n\nWait, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, losing things and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing... it was definitely a problem at home and with friends, too, not just at school.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and back in school. Looking at your life overall right now—not just work—would you say these attention and organization problems are causing you real difficulties in multiple areas, or is it mainly just the nursing job that's been affected?\n\nPatient: Um, it’s everywhere, honestly, but work is where it’s really crashing down on me right now. The concerns there have gotten serious enough that I’m actually on formal supervision. The other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's humiliating. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, you know? \n\nBrenda is usually the one who has to check my work. She’s super sweet about it, but I still hate it. She actually just got back from an Alaskan cruise last week and brought some smoked salmon into the break room. She showed me all these pictures of the glaciers from her balcony, and it looked absolutely freezing. I don't think I could ever do a cold-weather vacation. I despise being cold. I actually bought this massive, heavy-duty down puffer coat last winter from REI—like, the kind that goes all the way down to your ankles—just because I got assigned to the satellite parking lot at the hospital and have to wait outside. \n\nYou have to take this little shuttle bus from the lot to the main entrance, and the heater on it is always broken. The driver, Gary, is hilarious, though. He always wears these vintage aviator sunglasses and plays classic rock on the radio at like, six in the morning. We were listening to Fleetwood Mac the other day. *Rumours* is probably one of the best albums ever recorded. My dad had it on vinyl when I was growing up, and I always loved the album cover with that weird little crystal ball thing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm totally rambling. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's just the nursing job. I mean, it messes up my personal life too, but work is just the most stressful part right now because of the whole supervision thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the work supervision is really stressful. Outside of work—at home, in your relationships, managing your finances, or other personal responsibilities—are you having significant problems because of losing track of things and forgetting what you need to do, or is it more manageable in those areas?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s definitely not manageable at home either. It’s honestly a disaster everywhere. I constantly forget things that should just be completely routine. Like, I’ll completely forget to pay a bill at home, or I'll forget to call a family member back. My sister got so mad at me last week because I didn't call her back about planning her baby shower. She's having a boy, which is super exciting because they already have two girls. \n\nThe girls are adorable, they do competitive cheerleading now. I did gymnastics when I was little, but I had to quit because I broke my wrist falling off the balance beam. I had to wear this neon pink cast for like six weeks in the third grade, and it got so gross and itchy inside. My mom used to let me stick a long knitting needle down there to scratch it, which you are definitely not supposed to do—as a nurse, I know that now. I actually had a patient come up to the med-surg unit with a fresh cast a few weeks ago, and it totally brought me back to that weird fiberglass smell. \n\nBut um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my personal life and forgetting things. Yeah, it's bad. I'll forget to follow up on a missing order, or I'll just completely fail to show up for a doctor's appointment or a haircut. And the most frustrating part is that I really meant to do it! I'll even set a reminder on my phone, and it just doesn't matter. The reminder goes off, I swipe it away thinking, 'I'll do that in literally two seconds,' and then my brain just totally deletes it.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on something—like reading a book, watching a movie, or having a conversation—do you find yourself feeling antsy or like you need to get up and move around, even if there's no real reason to?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't get that antsy feeling where I need to move around. I can sit still for a movie or whatever. I actually love just sitting on the couch. We just watched this huge marathon of those baking shows on Netflix last weekend. My boyfriend loves them, but he always falls asleep halfway through the episodes. He works construction, so he's up at like 4 AM every day, which is just insane to me. I absolutely need my sleep. \n\nWe actually just bought a new mattress because our old one was sagging so badly in the middle and it was making my lower back hurt on top of being on my feet all day at the hospital. It's one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box. It's crazy how they vacuum seal them! You cut the plastic and it just expands like a giant sponge. The delivery guy left it out in the rain, though, and the cardboard got completely mushy. Our front porch doesn't have an awning, which is something I really want to get fixed before winter. I saw this really cute striped one on Pinterest that would match our shutters perfectly, but I don't know how much it costs to install something like that... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, getting up and moving around. No, I don't really have a problem with sitting still. I'm usually perfectly fine just staying put.\n\nDoctor: You've told me about losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and since childhood. But I want to ask you directly: because of these attention and memory problems, have you actually lost a job, failed a class, had a serious relationship end, or faced other major consequences? Or is it more that it's frustrating and annoying, but you've managed to keep things together overall?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before, the work thing is pretty major. I haven't actually been fired, but I am on that formal supervision right now. The other nurses are literally having to double-check my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's terrible. I feel like a total liability to the unit instead of a leader. \n\nBrenda usually checks my charts, and she's so nice about it, but it still makes me feel awful. She actually brought in some more of that smoked salmon yesterday. Her husband caught it on their trip, and he apparently has this huge smoker setup in their backyard. He built it himself out of an old oil drum, which is kind of genius. My boyfriend wants to get a smoker, but we barely have any room on our patio right now because of all my potted plants. I went a little crazy at the nursery this spring and bought like six different kinds of tomatoes. \n\nI don't even really like raw tomatoes that much, but I love making homemade salsa. The secret is roasting the jalapeños first so they get that nice char on them. I burned my finger doing it last time, though, right on the oven rack. I had this nasty blister for a week, and it made putting on my sterile gloves at work an absolute nightmare. The powder inside the gloves just irritated it so badly... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again. I'm totally off track. You were asking about major consequences, right? Yeah, I haven't lost the job yet, but the supervision and feeling like a liability... it's definitely way past just being annoying. It's a real problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, definitely not. Like I said before, I absolutely need my sleep. I never get those bursts of energy where I don't need to rest. Honestly, I'm usually just exhausted. Or do you act without thinking things through first?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't feel like I have to be moving, but I mean, as a nurse, I'm literally always walking anyway. They have these huge, thick soles. Everyone on the unit wears them now. \n\nI used to be a strict Dansko clog girl, but they're just so heavy, and my dog actually chewed the heel off my last pair. I just space out a lot, mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child or teenager, before you took on the charge-nurse role, did you have similar problems with focus and attention, or did these difficulties really start around the time you took on those expanded responsibilities two years ago?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's definitely been there since I was a kid. Like, even in elementary school, my teachers would always write on my report cards that I was bright but careless. I was constantly losing my worksheets or just completely forgetting to turn in my homework. My teachers were always writing on my report cards that I was, you know, bright but careless. I'd lose my worksheets constantly, or I'd do the homework and just completely forget to turn it in. The teachers never said I was bouncing off the walls or anything like that. It was always just the daydreaming, losing my pencils, that kind of thing. I actually loved school supplies, though. Every August, my mom would take me to Staples, and I'd pick out those Lisa Frank folders. Do you remember those? With the neon dolphins and the golden retrievers? They were so cool. Like I was saying, I was the quiet one. I'd just sit at my desk, totally zoned out. We had these desks where the top lifted up, and you could keep all your stuff inside them. I used to organize my Lisa Frank folders and my gel pens in there, but it always ended up being a total mess by the end of the week. She had this massive grandfather clock in the hallway that chimed every hour, and it used to scare the life out of me when I was little. The frosting they use is just incredible, it's cream cheese based, I think. I just wasn't mentally in the room half the time.\n\nDoctor: Besides school, when you were a kid at home or playing with friends, did you also have trouble keeping track of things, losing your belongings, or forgetting what you were supposed to be doing? The hospital where I work has the absolute worst Wi-Fi. I'm a nurse on the med-surg floor, and our charting system is always crashing because the connection drops. It makes my job so much harder because I'm already struggling to keep track of all my patients' meds and when they need their vitals taken. I use this giant clipboard with all these different colored sticky notes just to keep everything straight, but sometimes the notes fall off. Last week, I found one stuck to the bottom of my shoe when I got home. \n\nOh! And I have to buy these specific nursing shoes because I'm on my feet for twelve hours, and my arches are terrible. I ordered a new pair online, but I put in my old apartment address by accident. I had to drive over there to see if the new tenants had my package, and they were so weird about it. They had this massive dog that just kept barking at me through the window. It looked like a Mastiff mix or something. I love dogs, but big ones kind of freak me out ever since I got knocked over by a Golden Retriever at the park when I was little. \n\nWait, sorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, losing things and forgetting what I was supposed to be doing... it was definitely a problem at home and with friends, too, not just at school.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and back in school. Looking at your life overall right now—not just work—would you say these attention and organization problems are causing you real difficulties in multiple areas, or is it mainly just the nursing job that's been affected?\n\nPatient: Um, it’s everywhere, honestly, but work is where it’s really crashing down on me right now. The concerns there have gotten serious enough that I’m actually on formal supervision. The other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's humiliating. It makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of a leader, you know? \n\nBrenda is usually the one who has to check my work. She’s super sweet about it, but I still hate it. She actually just got back from an Alaskan cruise last week and brought some smoked salmon into the break room. She showed me all these pictures of the glaciers from her balcony, and it looked absolutely freezing. I don't think I could ever do a cold-weather vacation. I despise being cold. I actually bought this massive, heavy-duty down puffer coat last winter from REI—like, the kind that goes all the way down to your ankles—just because I got assigned to the satellite parking lot at the hospital and have to wait outside. \n\nYou have to take this little shuttle bus from the lot to the main entrance, and the heater on it is always broken. The driver, Gary, is hilarious, though. He always wears these vintage aviator sunglasses and plays classic rock on the radio at like, six in the morning. We were listening to Fleetwood Mac the other day. *Rumours* is probably one of the best albums ever recorded. My dad had it on vinyl when I was growing up, and I always loved the album cover with that weird little crystal ball thing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm totally rambling. What did you ask me? Oh, right, if it's just the nursing job. I mean, it messes up my personal life too, but work is just the most stressful part right now because of the whole supervision thing.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the work supervision is really stressful. Outside of work—at home, in your relationships, managing your finances, or other personal responsibilities—are you having significant problems because of losing track of things and forgetting what you need to do, or is it more manageable in those areas?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s definitely not manageable at home either. It’s honestly a disaster everywhere. I constantly forget things that should just be completely routine. Like, I’ll completely forget to pay a bill at home, or I'll forget to call a family member back. My sister got so mad at me last week because I didn't call her back about planning her baby shower. She's having a boy, which is super exciting because they already have two girls. \n\nThe girls are adorable, they do competitive cheerleading now. I did gymnastics when I was little, but I had to quit because I broke my wrist falling off the balance beam. I had to wear this neon pink cast for like six weeks in the third grade, and it got so gross and itchy inside. My mom used to let me stick a long knitting needle down there to scratch it, which you are definitely not supposed to do—as a nurse, I know that now. I actually had a patient come up to the med-surg unit with a fresh cast a few weeks ago, and it totally brought me back to that weird fiberglass smell. \n\nBut um... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my personal life and forgetting things. Yeah, it's bad. I'll forget to follow up on a missing order, or I'll just completely fail to show up for a doctor's appointment or a haircut. And the most frustrating part is that I really meant to do it! I'll even set a reminder on my phone, and it just doesn't matter. The reminder goes off, I swipe it away thinking, 'I'll do that in literally two seconds,' and then my brain just totally deletes it.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down trying to focus on something—like reading a book, watching a movie, or having a conversation—do you find yourself feeling antsy or like you need to get up and move around, even if there's no real reason to?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't get that antsy feeling where I need to move around. I can sit still for a movie or whatever. I actually love just sitting on the couch. We just watched this huge marathon of those baking shows on Netflix last weekend. My boyfriend loves them, but he always falls asleep halfway through the episodes. He works construction, so he's up at like 4 AM every day, which is just insane to me. I absolutely need my sleep. \n\nWe actually just bought a new mattress because our old one was sagging so badly in the middle and it was making my lower back hurt on top of being on my feet all day at the hospital. It's one of those memory foam ones that comes rolled up in a box. It's crazy how they vacuum seal them! You cut the plastic and it just expands like a giant sponge. The delivery guy left it out in the rain, though, and the cardboard got completely mushy. Our front porch doesn't have an awning, which is something I really want to get fixed before winter. I saw this really cute striped one on Pinterest that would match our shutters perfectly, but I don't know how much it costs to install something like that... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, getting up and moving around. No, I don't really have a problem with sitting still. I'm usually perfectly fine just staying put.\n\nDoctor: You've told me about losing things and forgetting tasks at work, at home, and since childhood. But I want to ask you directly: because of these attention and memory problems, have you actually lost a job, failed a class, had a serious relationship end, or faced other major consequences? Or is it more that it's frustrating and annoying, but you've managed to keep things together overall?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before, the work thing is pretty major. I haven't actually been fired, but I am on that formal supervision right now. The other nurses are literally having to double-check my charge-nurse follow-through, which is just... it's terrible. I feel like a total liability to the unit instead of a leader. \n\nBrenda usually checks my charts, and she's so nice about it, but it still makes me feel awful. She actually brought in some more of that smoked salmon yesterday. Her husband caught it on their trip, and he apparently has this huge smoker setup in their backyard. He built it himself out of an old oil drum, which is kind of genius. My boyfriend wants to get a smoker, but we barely have any room on our patio right now because of all my potted plants. I went a little crazy at the nursery this spring and bought like six different kinds of tomatoes. \n\nI don't even really like raw tomatoes that much, but I love making homemade salsa. The secret is roasting the jalapeños first so they get that nice char on them. I burned my finger doing it last time, though, right on the oven rack. I had this nasty blister for a week, and it made putting on my sterile gloves at work an absolute nightmare. The powder inside the gloves just irritated it so badly... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again. I'm totally off track. You were asking about major consequences, right? Yeah, I haven't lost the job yet, but the supervision and feeling like a liability... it's definitely way past just being annoying. It's a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods—maybe lasting a few days or longer—where you felt unusually energetic, your thoughts were racing really fast, you needed less sleep than usual, or you felt like you could do a lot more than you normally can?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. Like I said before, I absolutely need my sleep. I never get those bursts of energy where I don't need to rest. Honestly, I'm usually just exhausted. Though I do try to drink a lot of coffee to keep up. We actually got this incredibly fancy espresso machine for Christmas from my parents. It has all these complicated pressure dials and a steam wand that I still have no idea how to use properly. \n\nMy brother is the one who really knows how to use it—he used to work at this little indie coffee shop downtown when he was in college. They had the most amazing almond croissants there. I actually tried making croissants from scratch once, and let me tell you, laminating dough is an absolute nightmare. You have to keep folding the cold butter in, and if your kitchen gets even slightly too warm, the butter just melts and leaks everywhere in the oven and ruins the whole batch. It took me like three whole days to make them, and they ended up looking like sad, flat little crescent rolls anyway. I think I used the wrong type of flour, honestly, and I read later you're supposed to use European butter because it has a higher fat content... \n\nWait, sorry. I'm doing it again, aren't I? What were you asking about? Oh, right, the energy thing and racing thoughts. No, I've never had anything like that where I don't need sleep. I honestly wish I had a little more energy most days, but no, nothing like what you're describing."
